Lead-in chamfers at a mating mouth sound\n   helpful and are not. They make tiny compound facets that print badly on someone\n   else's machine.\n4. **Never polish a concave edge.** A cosmetic chamfer on an inside corner adds a thin\n   wedge instead of taking a corner off: it prints as a feather edge and collects stress\n   where the part is already weakest. A concave junction that needs relieving gets a\n   deliberate 3mm structural chamfer, sized for the load, before the polish pass runs.\n   `is_convex` and `concave_edges` are in the vocabulary a part file gets, because this\n   is the one polish mistake that is invisible in code: the selector reads as an\n   ordinary exposed-edge query and the part builds, exports and prints without\n   complaint. It shipped in this library once.\n5. **Select chamfer edges by filtering, never blanket-chamfer.** Mating edges, back-face\n   edges, bottom-face edges and concave edges all have to be excluded.\n   **Filter for what must not be touched, then let the kernel refuse the rest.** A\n   chamfer call is all or nothing, so one edge that cannot land takes the whole pass\n   down, and the tempting response is to keep narrowing the set by hand until it builds.\n   That is how a part ends up with three chamfered edges out of ninety. Narrow the set\n   for reasons you can name, then chamfer greedily: try the set, and where it fails,\n   bisect and keep the halves that build. Refuse any batch that makes a face smaller\n   than the corner triangle three chamfers leave, about `0.866 * size ** 2`, since\n   anything smaller is chamfers colliding. Chamfer the original solid with the whole\n   accepted set each time, never the result of the last attempt: an edge belongs to the\n   shape it was selected from, and applying batches in sequence quietly re-chamfers the\n   first body and returns it.\n6. **No text labels on parts.** File naming carries catalog identity. There is a second\n   reason beyond taste: a glyph's outline comes from a system font, so a part with text\n   on it builds to different geometry on a different machine. The calibration coupon,\n   which needs a label because four of them come off one plate looking identical, comes\n   out at 2600.6mm3 with 88 faces on one machine and 2601.0 with 83 on another. Nothing\n   about its fit moves, but its card cannot be asserted anywhere but where it was\n   written.\n7. **Consistency is the polish.** One chamfer size across a family is what makes it read\n   as a designed system rather than a pile of prints.\n\n**Sliver check after every polish pass.** Faces under 1mm² are a polish bug unless\njustified. The only ones the doctrine allows outright are the corner triangles where\nthree 1mm chamfers meet at a convex corner, about 0.87mm². Anything else is usually\nchamfers colliding near a mating mouth, or a chamfered concave edge. A part declares the\ncount it has earned on its card, so a new one is a regression and a known one is silent.\n\n## Kernel rules\n\nSpecific to OCCT, and measured on real parts rather than reasoned about.\n\n- **Two chamfered convex edges need more than `2 * chamfer_size` of face between them.**\n  Closer and OCCT raises `BRep_API: command not done`, which build123d surfaces as\n  \"Failed creating a chamfer, try a smaller length value(s)\". At 1mm the shelf fails\n  with 1.66mm between the edges and builds with 2.16mm; at 0.5mm it builds with 1.16mm.\n  The threshold tracks the chamfer exactly. This is the single most common way a part\n  stops building, so `chamfer` says this much at the point of failure rather than\n  leaving the kernel's advice to stand: taking \"try a smaller length\" at its word is how\n  a part ends up with a 0.4mm chamfer that lands and then prints as a defect.\n- **One chamfered edge needs more than `chamfer_size` of face**, which is the same rule\n  with one chamfer instead of two and is easy to miss because it looks like plenty of\n  room. It bites wherever a polished edge sits beside something that is never polished:\n  a concave junction, a structural chamfer's toe, a pocket wall. Measured on three\n  different parts, all at the same threshold: 1.08mm builds and 1.00mm fails at a 1mm\n  chamfer.\n- **A batch chamfer failure is not a bad edge.** Every edge in a failing set chamfers\n  fine on its own, so testing them one at a time reports that nothing is wrong. Bisect\n  the set pairwise instead. \"Try a smaller length\" would never have found the rule above.\n- **The second way a chamfer dies, where bisecting does not help**, is an edge whose end\n  lands on a vertex with four faces around it and only three edges between them. Two of\n  those faces touch at a point without sharing an edge, and OCCT has no cap for that\n  corner. The edge fails on its own, on a clean body, at every length down to 0.05mm, so\n  nothing about the failure looks like a clearance problem. The fix is to chamfer in two\n  passes so that the first one gives those two faces a real edge to meet along, then\n  reselect from the result. Found on the parts bin, where the front drop and the side\n  taper land at the same height by design.\n- **Prefer `new_edges` over geometric selectors.** Each chamfer changes topology, so a\n  selector resolved against pristine geometry drifts once an earlier chamfer runs.\n  `new_edges(before, combined=after)` returns exactly the edges an operation created. It\n  is the algebra-mode equivalent of a builder's `Select.LAST`, and algebra mode is what a\n  part file uses, so `part.edges() - last` is not available. It is what makes a part\n  survive flexing its counts.\n- **A loft through three or more sections wanders unless `ruled=True`.** With two sections the flag changes nothing, measured to the same volume, so a plain taper is fine either way. At three, the smooth default fits one spline through all of them and the wall between sections is nowhere the numbers say: lofting a 20mm square through a 10mm waist, the wall halfway there reads 12.5mm across where the straight line says 15. Every wall thickness and overhang worked out from the section dimensions drifts by that much, silently, between the stations. `ruled=True` puts the wall exactly on the straight line between sections, which is why the gridfinity shelf builds as one five-section ruled loft. Mismatched sections are not the hazard they look like: a square lofts to a hexagon or to its own 45 degree twin without complaint, so there is no vertex-matching rule, only the spline one.\n- **A ruled loft between polygons polishes like a prism; a smooth loft to a curve does not.** The rim where a square meets a circle refuses a 1mm chamfer and accepts 0.5, and taking the kernel's \"smaller length\" advice there walks straight under the 0.8mm floor. When a lofted part is going to be polished, prefer ruled polygonal sections; a rim that has to be round anyway is `crown`'s case, not a shrinking chamfer's.\n- **Loft does not police its sections.** Two sections landing nearly in one plane, the shape a `Pos` typo makes, return a fraction-of-a-mm3 sliver solid instead of raising. It builds, exports and passes the one-solid check, so the bounding box and the render are what catch it. A loft whose result looks wrong by eye is wrong at a section, not at the surface.\n- **A Fusion workaround is sometimes a real constraint wearing a disguise.** The gusset\n  drop that existed only to escape `ASM_BL_NO_MATE` turned out to be the same \"a chamfer\n  needs room to land\" constraint at a different number. Deleting it would have been\n  wrong; restating it as a rule was right.\n\n## Cards\n\nA part file carries geometry, not rationale. Every part worth keeping has a card next to\nit, same basename. **Read the card before editing the part, and update it when done.**\n\nFour sections, and one of them does work nothing else does:\n\n- `## What it is`\n- `## Design notes`, why the key dimensions are what they are\n- `## Don't`, **required**, what was tried and rejected. Geometry records what is; only\n  this records what is deliberately absent. Without it an agent helpfully re-adds the\n  lead-in chamfer that was retired on purpose.\n- `## Changelog`, dated\n\nPlus two machine-facing pieces:\n\n- The **AUTO block**, regenerated by `nurb card`. Never hand-edit it. It is a cache, not\n  a source of truth, and it holds no timestamp so regenerating it on unchanged geometry\n  produces no diff.\n- The **accepted baselines**, a TOML fence carrying what this part has already justified:\n  its sliver count, its real minimum wall, its printer settings. The number goes next to\n  the sentence that earns it, because a count on its own is a magic number. Machine facts\n  stay out of it: a bed size belongs to the machine, so it lives in `printer.toml` at the\n  project root, which names a shipped profile (`profile = \"bambu_a1_mini\"`) and can\n  override any check setting machine-wide. A printer is really a fact about the workshop,\n  not the project, so `~/.config/nurb/config.toml` takes the same schema and covers every\n  project on the machine; `printer.toml` overrides it where they disagree.\n\n```toml\n[part]\nmin_wall = 1.0\nforward = [-1, 0, 0]\n\n[accepted]\nsliver = 6\n```\n\n### Variants\n\nA catalog entry that is the same function at different numbers is a variant, not a new\nfile. It goes in the same fence, and `build`, `check`, `card` and `export` all walk it\nexactly as they walk the part's own defaults, so it gets its own STL, its own baselines\nand its own line in the AUTO block.\n\n```toml\n[variants.shelf_gridfinity_3x2]\nnote = \"The same shelf sized for the wide bin: three columns instead of two.\"\n\n[variants.shelf_gridfinity_3x2.params]\ngrid_x = 3\n","total_lines":529}},"locations":[]}

{"type":"tool_call_update","toolCallId":"call-e5754052-ead2-49f6-9d18-add19c95b392-17","status":"completed","content":[{"type":"content","content":{"type":"text","text":"1→\"\"\"Support-free counterbores: the stepped bridging hole.\n\nA counterbored hole prints mouth toward the bed, because that is where the screw head\ncomes from. That floats its ceiling: the shelf the head bears on is laid flat over the\nopen bore, and the smaller hole's first rim is a circle drawn on air. Slicers print it\nbadly or demand support inside a pocket nobody can clean.\n\nThe print-farm fix is sequential bridging, two sacrificial layers between bore and\nhole. First a slot the hole's width, bridged chord-to-chord across the bore; then the\n10→same slot turned ninety degrees, bridged across the first; then the round hole starts\nover the crossing, ringed by material that bridges the short way at every point. One edge that cannot land takes the whole call down, and\nthe natural response is to shrink the set by hand until it builds. That is how a part\nwith ninety exposed edges ends up with three chamfered ones, and it is why the same\ndoctrine produces far more chamfers in a GUI, where a human adds them one feature at a\ntime and works around each collision individually.\n\nThe doctrine describes this algorithm and used to leave every project to write it. It\n10→was 35 lines in one `system.py`, which meant the next project would write it again or,\nfar more likely, skip it and hand-narrow the set, which is exactly what the rule exists\nto prevent.\n\"\"\"\n\nimport collections.abc\nimport functools\n\nfrom build123d import chamfer as _chamfer\n\n20→# What OCCT says when a chamfer will not land. The same strings `nurb verify`\n# matches on, because both are asking the same question: did the kernel refuse, or did\n# the part refuse in its own words.\nKERNEL = (\"Failed creating a chamfer\", \"BRep_API\")\n\n\ndef _advice(count, size, word):\n    \"\"\"What the doctrine knows about this failure, said at the moment it happens.\n\n    The kernel's own message is \"try a smaller length value(s)\", and the doctrine is\n30→    explicit that following it would never have found the real rule: a chamfer fails\n    for room, not for length. Shrinking it is the one response that makes a part quietly\n    worse, because a 0.4mm chamfer lands and then reads as a defect on the print.\n\n    So the rule goes here rather than only in `nurb rules`. This is the most common way\n    a part stops building, and an error is the one place a reader is guaranteed to be\n    looking.\n    \"\"\"\n    lines = [\n        \"\",\n40→        f\"  A {word} fails for room, not for length, and shrinking it is usually the\",\n        f\"  wrong fix. Two {word}ed convex edges need more than {2 * size:g}mm of face between\",\n        f\"  them, and a single one needs more than {size:g}mm.\",\n    ]\n    if count > 1:\n        lines += [\n            \"\",\n            f\"  This call passed {count} edges as one batch, and a batch is all or nothing:\",\n            \"  one edge that cannot land takes the whole call down. Every edge in it very\",\n            \"  likely chamfers fine on its own, so testing them one at a time will report\",\n50→            \"  that nothing is wrong.\",\n            \"\",\n            # `!r` not `:g`: a size is a measurement, and this codebase reads a bare `1`\n            # as a count. Suggested code has to echo back the float the caller passed.\n            f\"  polish(shape, edges, {size!r}) bisects the set for you and keeps the halves that\",\n            \"  build, which is what the doctrine prescribes instead of narrowing by hand.\",\n        ]\n    else:\n        lines += [\n            \"\",\n60→            \"  A single edge that fails at every length is the other case: its end lands on\",\n            \"  a vertex with four faces and only three edges between them, and OCCT has no\",\n            \"  cap for that corner. An edge belongs to the shape it was selected from\n    and `chamfer` reads its target off that shape, so applying batches in sequence would\n    quietly keep re-chamfering the first body and hand it back.\n\n    This does not choose what to polish. Deciding which edges are exposed, which are\n    mating geometry and which are concave is the part's judgement and stays in the part.\n120→    \"\"\"\n    floor = 0.8 * size**2\n\n    def tiny(solid):\n        return len([f for f in solid.faces() if f.area < floor])\n\n    allowance = tiny(shape)\n    kept, best = [], shape\n\n    def lands(candidate):\n130→        try:\n            # The raw kernel call, not the guarded one: bisecting expects failures and\n            # discards them, so building the guidance text here would be advice nobody\n            # reads, once per attempt.\n            out = _chamfer(candidate, size)\n        except Exception:\n            return None\n        return out if tiny(out) <= allowance else None\n\n    def take(batch):\n140→        nonlocal kept, best\n        out = lands(kept + batch)\n        if out is not None:\n            kept, best = kept + batch, out\n            return\n        if len(batch) == 1:\n            return  # this one cannot land beside what is already there\n        half = len(batch) // 2\n        take(batch[:half])\n        take(batch[half:])\n150→\n    take(sorted(edges, key=lambda e: (-e.length, e.center().X, e.center().Y, e.center().Z)))\n    return best\n"}}],"rawOutput":{"type":"ReadFile","FileContent":{"content":"1→\"\"\"The polish pass: chamfer as much of a set as the kernel will take.\n\n`chamfer` is all or nothing. The convexity test is here because a polish pass cannot\n# be written without it: chamfering an inside corner is the one polish mistake that looks\n# fine in code. `measured` is here because the alternative to asking for a dimension is\n# inventing one, and an invented one builds, checks clean and prints.\n# `stand` is here because a diagonal print variant cannot be written without it: the\n# rotation is trivial, but the bed facet it cuts is a doctrine rule (2mm of flat, or the\n# first layer is one extrusion line wide and peels), and a part file left to tilt by\n# hand ships the knife edge.\n# `crown` is here because rounding a variable-height rim by hand is an eight-step sweep\n40→# construction (issue #55), and every direct fillet of such a rim dies in OCCT's corner\n# capping. It is the doctrine's one sanctioned round-edge treatment.\n# `counterbore` is here because the naive alternative is two cylinders that build,\n# check as an ordinary bridge, and print a screw seat on sagging air: the stepped\n# bridging stack is fixed print-farm practice, not a judgement, so it is generated.\n# `reject` is here because a part that knows a configuration cannot work (a hole\n# narrower than the tool it holds) has to be able to say so, and the alternative is a\n# bare ValueError that the viewer can only present as a crash. A refusal through\n# `reject` keeps its message and the parameter it names, and is shown as a limit of\n# the design.\n50→# `chamfer` deliberately shadows build123d's. Same behaviour, same exception type;\n# what it adds is the doctrine's rule on the way out of a failure, because the kernel's\n# own advice is \"try a smaller length value(s)\" and following it is how a part ends up\n# with a 0.4mm chamfer that lands and prints as a defect. It is the most common way a\n# part stops building, so it is the one message worth owning. `fillet` is deliberately\n# not wrapped: build123d's fillet error already names `max_fillet()` and a smaller\n# radius really is the fix there, so the chamfer rule would be wrong half the time.\n# `import_stl` shadows build123d's for a blunter reason: build123d's returns a `Face`,\n# a sheet of triangles with no inside, and subtracting from one segfaults instead of\n# raising. A part builds inside the `nurb dev` process, so the whole watcher dies\n60→# without printing anything. It is also the first name a model tries when a downloaded\n# file lands in a project. The convexity test is here because a polish pass cannot\n# be written without it: chamfering an inside corner is the one polish mistake that looks\n# fine in code. `measured` is here because the alternative to asking for a dimension is\n# inventing one, and an invented one builds, checks clean and prints.\n# `stand` is here because a diagonal print variant cannot be written without it: the\n# rotation is trivial, but the bed facet it cuts is a doctrine rule (2mm of flat, or the\n# first layer is one extrusion line wide and peels), and a part file left to tilt by\n# hand ships the knife edge.\n# `crown` is here because rounding a variable-height rim by hand is an eight-step sweep\n40→# construction (issue #55), and every direct fillet of such a rim dies in OCCT's corner\n# capping. It is the doctrine's one sanctioned round-edge treatment.\n# `counterbore` is here because the naive alternative is two cylinders that build,\n# check as an ordinary bridge, and print a screw seat on sagging air: the stepped\n# bridging stack is fixed print-farm practice, not a judgement, so it is generated.\n# `reject` is here because a part that knows a configuration cannot work (a hole\n# narrower than the tool it holds) has to be able to say so, and the alternative is a\n# bare ValueError that the viewer can only present as a crash. A refusal through\n# `reject` keeps its message and the parameter it names, and is shown as a limit of\n# the design.\n50→# `chamfer` deliberately shadows build123d's. Same behaviour, same exception type;\n# what it adds is the doctrine's rule on the way out of a failure, because the kernel's\n# own advice is \"try a smaller length value(s)\" and following it is how a part ends up\n# with a 0.4mm chamfer that lands and prints as a defect. It is the doctrine's one sanctioned round-edge treatment.\n# `counterbore` is here because the naive alternative is two cylinders that build,\n# check as an ordinary bridge, and print a screw seat on sagging air: the stepped\n# bridging stack is fixed print-farm practice, not a judgement, so it is generated.\n# `reject` is here because a part that knows a configuration cannot work (a hole\n# narrower than the tool it holds) has to be able to say so, and the alternative is a\n# bare ValueError that the viewer can only present as a crash. A refusal through\n# `reject` keeps its message and the parameter it names, and is shown as a limit of\n# the design.\n50→# `chamfer` deliberately shadows build123d's. {"type":"tool_call_update","toolCallId":"call-e2440d33-0441-4229-b4be-d471f61e2ce7-25","status":"completed","content":[{"type":"content","content":{"type":"text","text":"1→\"\"\"Named real-world values, with provenance.\n\nThe bottleneck on a one-off part is not geometry, it is dimensions. An agent cannot\nmeasure a freezer shelf, and a guessed number produces a perfect model of the wrong\nobject: it builds, it checks clean, and it prints. Nothing downstream catches it.\n\nSo a measurement is a named value on disk with a note saying how it was obtained, and\nasking for one that is not on file raises. That failure is the feature. It is the moment\nto ask rather than to pick something plausible.\n10→\"\"\"\n\nimport pathlib\nimport sys\nimport tomllib\n\nFILENAME = \"measurements.toml\"\n\n\nclass MeasurementError(LookupError):\n20→    \"\"\"A measurement is missing or unusable.\n\n    Not `KeyError`, which reprs its argument: a message with a worked example in it comes\n    out as one quoted string full of backslash-n, and the example is the useful part.\n    \"\"\"\n\n\ndef _caller_dir(depth):\n    \"\"\"Where the code that called us lives, so the lookup needs no configuration.\"\"\"\n    frame = sys._getframe(depth)\n30→    here = frame.f_globals.get(\"__file__\")\n    return pathlib.Path(here).resolve().parent if here else pathlib.Path.cwd()\n\n\ndef _find(start):\n    \"\"\"The nearest measurements file, without leaving the project.\n\n    The walk stops at the project root, meaning the directory holding `parts/`. Without\n    that stop it would keep going to the filesystem root and could silently answer with\n    some unrelated file from an ancestor directory, which is the exact failure this whole\n40→    module exists to prevent: a dimension that is wrong and looks fine.\n    \"\"\"\n    for d in [start, *start.parents]:\n        if (d / FILENAME).is_file():\n            return d / FILENAME\n        if (d / \"parts\").is_dir():\n            return None\n    return None\n\n\n50→def _read(path):\n    \"\"\"Parse it, and say which file when it will not parse. Run\n    over a concave one it does the opposite: it adds a thin wedge into the corner,\n    which prints as a feather edge and collects stress where the part is already\n    weakest. A concave junction that needs relieving wants a deliberate structural\n    chamfer sized for the load, not the polish pass.\n1460→\n    Found by shape rather than by trying to identify chamfers: a strip about as wide as\n    the polish pass makes, whose long edges are all concave, is one. Width comes from\n    area over perimeter, which is the honest measure for a strip and does not care how\n    the strip is oriented.\n\n    **About as wide as the polish pass makes, not merely narrow.** A chamfer of length s\n    across a right-angled corner leaves a strip s * sqrt(2) wide, so the window is that\n    figure with a little headroom, and the limit was twice it until two parts showed\n    what twice costs. A 2mm structural chamfer, which is what the doctrine prescribes\n1470→    for a concave junction in thin material, leaves a 2.6mm strip; the 2mm of wall left\n    standing between two of them measures 1.9mm. Both sat under a 2.8mm limit, so the\n    rule fired six times at `mount_tape_measure` and again at `mount_akrobin_rail`, at\n    the exact geometry its own message tells you to use. A deliberate relief is bigger\n    than polish, and being bigger is the only thing that distinguishes it.\n\n    The cost is a polish chamfer in a shallow concave junction, past about 105 degrees,\n    where the strip a 1mm chamfer leaves is wide enough to read as deliberate. That is\n    the right way round to be wrong: `polish_edges` vetoes concave edges outright, so\n    this rule is the backstop for a part that rolls its own selector, and a backstop\n1480→    that cries wolf at correct geometry gets switched off.\n    \"\"\"\n    neighbours = edge_faces(shape)\n    limit = ctx.cosmetic_chamfer * 1.42 * 1.15\n    found = []\n    for face in shape.faces():\n        if face.geom_type != GeomType.PLANE:\n            continue\n        perimeter = sum(e.length for e in face.edges())\n        if perimeter <= 0:\n1490→            continue\n        width = 2 * face.area / perimeter\n        if width > limit:\n            continue\n        long_edges = [e for e in face.edges() if e.length > width * 1.5]\n        if len(long_edges) < 2:\n            continue\n        here = face.normal_at(face.center())\n        verdicts, oblique = [], []\n        for edge in long_edges:\n1500→            pair = neighbours.get(edge, [])\n            if len(pair) != 2:\n                continue\n            verdicts.append(is_convex(edge, *pair))\n            # Compared by topology, not by `is`: shape.faces() hands back fresh\n            # objects each call, so identity would always pick this face itself and\n            # every junction would look oblique.\n            other = pair[1] if pair[0] == face else pair[0]\n            # A bevel leans against what it joins. That is\n    the right way round to be wrong: `polish_edges` vetoes concave edges outright, so\n    this rule is the backstop for a part that rolls its own selector, and a backstop\n    that cries wolf at correct geometry gets switched off.\n    \"\"\"\n    neighbours = edge_faces(shape)\n    limit = ctx.cosmetic_chamfer * 1.42 * 1.15\n    found = []\n    for face in shape.faces():\n        if face.geom_type != GeomType.PLANE:\n            continue\n        perimeter = sum(e.length for e in face.edges())\n        if perimeter <= 0:\n            continue\n        width = 2 * face.area / perimeter\n        if width > limit:\n            continue\n        long_edges = [e for e in face.edges() if e.length > width * 1.5]\n        if len(long_edges) < 2:\n            continue\n        here = face.normal_at(face.center())\n        verdicts, oblique = [], []\n        for edge in long_edges:\n            pair = neighbours.get(edge, [])\n            if len(pair) != 2:\n                continue\n            verdicts.append(is_convex(edge, *pair))\n            # Compared by topology, not by `is`: shape.faces() hands back fresh\n            # objects each call, so identity would always pick this face itself and\n            # every junction would look oblique.\n            other = pair[1] if pair[0] == face else pair[0]\n            # A bevel leans against what it joins. A pocket floor is square to its\n            # walls, and a pocket is not polish however narrow it is.\n            oblique.append(abs(here.dot(other.normal_at(edge.center()))) > 0.3)\n        if verdicts and not any(verdicts) and oblique and all(oblique):\n            found.append(\n                Finding(\n                    \"concave_cosmetic\",\n                    WARN,\n                    f\"{width:.1f}mm strip sitting in a concave junction over \"\n                    f\"{face.area:.1f}mm2, which is polish where a structural chamfer belongs\",\n                    plain=f\"a {width:.1f}mm cosmetic chamfer tucked into an inside \"\n                    \"corner, where it thins the joint instead of tidying an edge\",\n                    value=round(width, 2),\n                    where=tuple(round(v, 2) for v in face.center()),\n                )\n            )\n    return found\n\n\ndef _probes(face, grid=2):\n    \"\"\"Points across a face with the outward normal at each.\"\"\"\n    spots = [face.center()]\n    if face.geom_type != GeomType.PLANE or face.area > 25:\n","total_lines":1668}},"locations":[]}

Bits drop in shank-first and stand there.\n\n## Design notes\n\n- `shank_diameter` is the measured shank (6.0mm). Pockets are that plus 0.3mm of\n  clearance, so 6.3mm, 12mm deep, with 3mm of solid floor under them.\n- Pitch is pocket diameter plus 2mm of wall, and the same 2mm runs from the outermost\n  pocket walls to the sides, so the default block is 43.5 × 18.6 × 15.\n- Pocket mouths and the top outer perimeter get a 0.8 × 45° chamfer. The bottom\n  perimeter stays sharp so the bounding box is the block as it sits.\n\n## Don't\n\n- Do not chamfer the bottom perimeter or the vertical corners: the stated bounding\n  box has to stay exact, and a bed-side chamfer is a `bed_bevel`.\n- Do not put a lead-in on the pocket floors or otherwise intrude into the bore.\n- Do not polish at 1mm: the specified chamfer is 0.8, and only on the mouths and the\n  top outer rim.\n- Do not roof the pockets or print them mouth-down.\n\n## Changelog\n\n- 2026-08-25: first cut, 5×2 grid derived from shank_diameter and columns.\n"},"content":[],"locations":[]}

Bits drop in shank-first and stand there.\n\n## Design notes\n\n- `shank_diameter` is the measured shank (6.0mm). Pockets are that plus 0.3mm of\n  clearance, so 6.3mm, 12mm deep, with 3mm of solid floor under them.\n- Pitch is pocket diameter plus 2mm of wall, and the same 2mm runs from the outermost\n  pocket walls to the sides, so the default block is 43.5 × 18.6 × 15.\n- Pocket mouths and the top outer perimeter get a 0.8 × 45° chamfer. The bottom\n  perimeter stays sharp so the bounding box is the block as it sits.\n\n## Don't\n\n- Do not chamfer the bottom perimeter or the vertical corners: the stated bounding\n  box has to stay exact, and a bed-side chamfer is a `bed_bevel`.\n- Do not put a lead-in on the pocket floors or otherwise intrude into the bore.\n- Do not polish at 1mm: the specified chamfer is 0.8, and only on the mouths and the\n  top outer rim.\n- Do not roof the pockets or print them mouth-down.\n\n## Changelog\n\n- 2026-08-25: first cut, 5×2 grid derived from shank_diameter and columns.\n"}],"rawOutput":null,"locations":[{"path":"parts/bit_block.md"}]}

across a bit shank; each pocket is this plus 0.3mm of clearance\n    columns: how many pockets sit along the long side\n    \"\"\"\n    clearance = 0.3\n    wall = 2.0\n    rows = 2\n    pocket_depth = 12.0\n    floor_thickness = 3.0\n    lead_in = 0.8\n\n    if columns < 1:\n        reject(\"need at least one column of pockets\", param=\"columns\")\n\n    pocket_dia = shank_diameter + clearance\n Bits drop in shank-first and stand there.\n\n## Design notes\n\n- `shank_diameter` is the measured shank (6.0mm). Pockets are that plus 0.3mm of\n  clearance, so 6.3mm, 12mm deep, with 3mm of solid floor under them.\n- Pitch is pocket diameter plus 2mm of wall, and the same 2mm runs from the outermost\n  pocket walls to the sides, so the default block is 43.5 × 18.6 × 15.\n- Pocket mouths and the top outer perimeter get a 0.8 × 45° chamfer. Bits drop in shank-first and stand there.\n\n## Design notes\n\n- `shank_diameter` is the measured shank (6.0mm). Pockets are that plus 0.3mm of\n  clearance, so 6.3mm, 12mm deep, with 3mm of solid floor under them.\n- Pitch is pocket diameter plus 2mm of wall, and the same 2mm runs from the outermost\n20→  pocket walls to the sides, so the default block is 43.5 × 18.6 × 15.\n- Pocket mouths and the top outer perimeter get a 0.8 × 45° chamfer. The bottom\n  perimeter stays sharp so the bounding box is the block as it sits.\n\n## Don't\n\n- Do not chamfer the bottom perimeter or the vertical corners: the stated bounding\n  box has to stay exact, and a bed-side chamfer is a `bed_bevel`.\n- Do not put a lead-in on the pocket floors or otherwise intrude into the bore.\n- Do not polish at 1mm: the specified chamfer is 0.8, and only on the mouths and the\n30→  top outer rim.\n- Do not roof the pockets or print them mouth-down.\n\n## Changelog\n\n- 2026-08-25: first cut, 5×2 grid derived from shank_diameter and columns.\n"}}],"rawOutput":{"type":"ReadFile","FileContent":{"content":"1→# bit_block\n\n<!-- AUTO: regenerated by `nurb card`.
